COMPREHENSIVE MARKET ANALYSIS REPORT: MICRON TECHNOLOGY (MU)¶

Analysis Date: June 18, 2026

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY¶

Micron Technology (MU) is experiencing an exceptionally bullish trading environment driven by sustained AI-driven memory demand. The stock has reached record highs with analyst price targets substantially revised upward, reflecting confidence in the semiconductor "super-cycle." However, elevated valuations and potential market headwinds warrant careful consideration.

SECTION 1: MICRON TECHNOLOGY (MU) - COMPANY SPECIFIC ANALYSIS¶

A. Recent Stock Performance & Momentum¶

  • Current Trend: MU has been one of the hottest technology stocks in 2026, with continuous probing of all-time highs
  • Recent Catalyst: Apple CEO Tim Cook announced that price hikes for iPhone production will be "unavoidable," validating Micron's strong negotiating position in memory chip pricing
  • Analyst Sentiment: Fair value estimates have been dramatically upgraded from $584.62 to $866.60 per share, reflecting substantial analyst optimism

B. AI Super-Cycle Thesis¶

The core investment narrative for MU centers on whether the AI super-cycle has fundamentally broken semiconductor industry boom-bust cycles:

  1. Memory Demand Drivers:
  2. High-bandwidth memory (HBM) demand from AI infrastructure deployment
  3. DRAM shortage conditions supporting pricing power

  4. Data center buildouts accelerating globally

  5. Pricing Power Evidence:

  6. Apple's willingness to absorb price increases validates strong demand fundamentals
  7. Gene Munster (Deepwater Asset Management) highlighted that when Apple—the most operationally disciplined hardware company—cannot absorb full costs, it signals exceptional industry strength

  8. Record profit posting despite elevated spending on expansion

  9. Capital Allocation Strategy:

  10. Micron is spending billions on facility expansion
  11. This demonstrates management confidence in sustained demand
  12. Expansion capacity will service memory demand for years

C. Upcoming Earnings Event¶

  • Critical Timing: Micron earnings are imminent (described as "coming days")
  • Expectation Evolution: Sales and EPS expectations have evolved throughout the quarter
  • Market Sensitivity: Stock price could experience significant volatility post-earnings

SECTION 2: SEMICONDUCTOR SECTOR & COMPETITIVE LANDSCAPE¶

A. Broader Sector Strength¶

The entire semiconductor ecosystem is experiencing significant momentum:

  1. Sector Winners:
  2. Intel (INTC): Surged 10%+ after Trump announced Apple-Intel foundry partnership
  3. Qualcomm (QCOM): Jumped 3.4% on same announcement
  4. Equipment Suppliers: Lam Research, Applied Materials, Photronics, and others soaring on foundry validation

  5. Supporting Suppliers: Western Digital, FormFactor, Vishay Intertechnology, Monolithic Power Systems, Entegris elevated

  6. Market Validation:

  7. President Trump's announcement of the Intel-Apple foundry partnership represents major geopolitical/industrial policy validation
  8. Signals U.S. commitment to domestic semiconductor manufacturing
  9. Positive spillover effects for memory chip makers via increased ecosystem confidence

B. Competitive Positioning¶

  • MU competes in DRAM and NAND flash memory
  • Memory segment showing tighter supply dynamics vs. logic/foundry segments
  • Strong pricing environment provides competitive advantage

SECTION 3: MACROECONOMIC ENVIRONMENT¶

A. Market Dynamics¶

  1. S&P 500 Weekly Performance: Posted 0.9% weekly gain amid US-Iran Memorandum of Understanding
  2. Tech Sector Strength: Tech stocks climbed late-week with sector-wide strength
  3. Resilience Against Fed Fears: Markets defying potential rate hike concerns, focusing on strong fundamentals
  4. Valuation Concerns: Some analysts warn stock market is "flirting with a dangerous valuation trap"

B. Federal Reserve Policy Outlook¶

  1. Leadership Transition: Kevin Warsh is taking over the Fed
  2. Rate Outlook: Potential rate hikes later in 2026 remain on the table
  3. Inflation Target: Debate over whether 4% could become the new inflation target, creating uncertainty
  4. Market Impact: Fed policy decisions could impact tech sector positioning

C. Geopolitical Factors¶

  1. US-Iran Relations: Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) signed, reducing geopolitical risk premium
  2. China Trade/Semiconductor Policy: No major negative developments in the past week
  3. Domestic Industrial Policy: Trump administration actively supporting semiconductor manufacturing in the U.S.

D. AI Investment Bubble Concerns¶

  1. IPO Pipeline: $4 trillion question about whether OpenAI, Anthropic, and SpaceX can successfully navigate IPO markets
  2. Infrastructure Spending: Electricity constraints emerging as real bottleneck for AI expansion
  3. Valuation Risk: Tech stocks at potentially stretched valuations, particularly around AI-exposed names

SECTION 4: RISK ASSESSMENT¶

A. Company-Specific Risks¶

  1. Valuation Risk: Stock has reached "nose-bleed levels" with fair value estimates $866.60—further upside may be limited
  2. Earnings Execution Risk: Imminent earnings could trigger volatility if expectations not met
  3. Super-Cycle Sustainability: Debate whether AI demand truly breaks traditional semiconductor cyclicality
  4. Inventory Risk: Once capital expansion reaches capacity, memory supply may catch up with demand, pressuring prices

B. Market-Wide Risks¶

  1. Rate Hike Cycle: Fed potentially hiking rates later in 2026; high-valuation tech stocks most vulnerable
  2. Valuation Trap: Market warning about stretched valuations creating mean-reversion risk
  3. AI Capex Cycle Duration: Uncertainty about whether AI infrastructure spending can sustain 5+ years
  4. Profit-Taking: After strong 2025-2026 run, institutional investors may redistribute gains

C. Sector-Specific Risks¶

  1. Memory Price Deflation: Once DRAM/NAND supply catches up with demand, pricing power evaporates
  2. Competitive Pressure: Other memory makers (SK Hynix, Samsung) also expanding capacity
  3. Technology Transition: New memory architectures could render existing capacity investments obsolete

SECTION 5: ACTIONABLE INSIGHTS FOR TRADERS¶

Bullish Case (Support for BUY)¶

  • ✅ AI demand appears structural and multi-year
  • ✅ Apple's inability to absorb price increases validates strong pricing power
  • ✅ Analyst sentiment exceptionally bullish with 48% upward fair value revision
  • ✅ Memory supply genuinely tight; HBM demand exceptional
  • ✅ Geopolitical environment improving (Iran MOU); U.S. industrial policy supportive
  • ✅ Sector-wide strength validates memory demand trajectory

Bearish Case (Support for SELL/CAUTION)¶

  • ⚠️ Valuation at all-time highs with limited additional upside
  • ⚠️ Fed rate hikes later in 2026 pose headwind for growth stocks
  • ⚠️ Earnings execution risk with stock at "nose-bleed levels"
  • ⚠️ Market warnings about valuation traps and AI bubble risks
  • ⚠️ Super-cycle sustainability unproven; traditional boom-bust cycles may persist
  • ⚠️ Once capacity expansion reaches market, pricing power could deflate rapidly

Neutral Case (Support for HOLD)¶

  • ➡️ Wait for earnings results before major position changes
  • ➡️ Monitor Fed policy communications closely
  • ➡️ Track memory price trends (HBM, DRAM, NAND) for demand validation
  • ➡️ Watch competitive capacity announcements from SK Hynix and Samsung

CONCLUSION¶

Micron Technology (MU) presents a classic momentum-driven investment dilemma: exceptional near-term fundamentals (AI demand, pricing power) offset by stretched valuations and macro headwinds. The stock has already priced in substantial optimism around the AI super-cycle, as evidenced by the 48% analyst fair value upgrade.

Key Trading Considerations: 1. Timing Risk: Imminent earnings could trigger volatility in either direction 2. Valuation Risk: Current levels appear to reflect most positive scenarios 3. Fed Risk: Rate hike potential in H2 2026 creates headwind 4. Upside Potential: Limited if valuation already reflects AI super-cycle thesis 5. Downside Risk: Substantial if earnings disappoint or macro environment deteriorates

The data supports a cautious stance at current levels, despite strong fundamentals.

Fundamentals Analyst¶

COMPREHENSIVE FUNDAMENTAL ANALYSIS REPORT¶

Micron Technology, Inc. (MU)¶

Analysis Date: June 18, 2026¶

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY¶

Micron Technology, Inc. (MU) is experiencing a remarkable turnaround and strong recovery across all financial metrics as of Q3 FY2026 (ending February 28, 2026). The company demonstrates exceptional momentum with surging revenues, dramatically improved profitability, and robust cash generation. The semiconductor manufacturer is leveraging AI-driven demand tailwinds and a recovering memory chip market to deliver industry-leading financial performance.

Key Highlights: - Q3 FY2026 Revenue: $23.9 billion (178% YoY increase from Q3 FY2025) - Dramatic Profitability Recovery: Net income of $13.8 billion (TTM) vs. $778 million in FY2024 - Strong Cash Generation: $11.9 billion operating cash flow in Q3 FY2026 - Market Recovery: Memory chip pricing showing strong recovery and elevated demand - Balance Sheet Strengthening: Working capital improving, debt reduction ongoing

COMPANY PROFILE¶

Company Name: Micron Technology, Inc.
Ticker: MU
Exchange: NASDAQ (NMS)
Sector: Technology
Industry: Semiconductors (DRAM, NAND Flash)
Market Capitalization: $1.28 trillion (as of June 18, 2026)
Headquarters: Boise, Idaho, USA

Business Description¶

Micron Technology is one of the world's leading manufacturers of DRAM (Dynamic Random-Access Memory) and NAND Flash memory semiconductors. The company serves diverse end markets including data centers, cloud computing, mobile devices, consumer electronics, automotive, and IoT applications. MU is a critical supplier to major OEMs and benefits significantly from AI infrastructure buildout and cloud computing expansion.

RISKS & CHALLENGES¶

Market Risks¶

  1. Cyclical Downturn Risk: Memory chip industry historically cyclical; current cycle may be inflated
  2. Demand Normalization: Post-AI buildup phase could see moderation in growth rates
  3. Competitive Pressure: SK Hynix and Samsung could increase market share
  4. ASP (Average Selling Price) Risk: Price compression if supply normalizes faster than demand

Operational Risks¶

  1. Capex Requirements: Heavy ongoing capex ($24-28B annually) required to maintain competitiveness
  2. Fab Utilization: Underutilization if demand disappoints could pressure margins
  3. Supply Chain: Geopolitical tensions could disrupt operations or limit China sales
  4. Manufacturing Complexity: Advanced node manufacturing requires flawless execution

Financial Risks¶

  1. Debt Refinancing: Need to refinance maturing debt in potentially higher rate environment
  2. Working Capital: Growing receivables and inventory requiring efficient management
  3. Tax Normalization: Effective tax rate could increase from current 14.7% level
  4. Foreign Exchange: Significant non-US operations expose to currency fluctuations

Strategic Risks¶

  1. Technology Transition: Need to maintain advanced manufacturing capabilities
  2. Export Controls: US government restrictions on China shipments could impact revenue
  3. Customer Concentration: Heavy reliance on hyperscale data center customers
  4. Product Mix: NAND Flash exposure to smartphone market recovery uncertainty

CONCLUSION¶

Micron Technology, Inc. (MU) represents a compelling case study in semiconductor industry recovery and AI-driven demand acceleration. The company has executed a remarkable turnaround from FY2023's significant losses to FY2025's strong profitability and FY2026's exceptional performance.

Financial Strengths: - Fortress balance sheet with fortress liquidity - Exceptional profitability and margins - Strong cash generation and free cash flow - Strategic positioning in AI infrastructure buildout - Disciplined capital allocation

Key Concerns: - Elevated valuations despite reasonable forward multiples - Cyclical industry exposure with potential for normalization - Heavy capex requirements ongoing - Geopolitical and supply chain risks - Sustainability of current growth rates uncertain

Overall Assessment: MU demonstrates excellent fundamental financial health, exceptional recent performance, and strategic positioning for continued growth. However, investors should be cognizant of valuation levels, industry cyclicality, and the risks inherent in the semiconductor business. The company's execution and financial discipline have been exemplary, making it a high-quality business at potentially elevated valuations.

1. THE "137% EPS GROWTH" SLEIGHT OF HAND—AND WHY IT'S ACTUALLY JUSTIFIED¶

But here's what the bear conveniently ignored:

Period EPS Estimate Context

2. THE SEMICONDUCTOR CYCLE ARGUMENT: THIS TIME IS DIFFERENT (AND I'LL PROVE IT)¶

The bear invoked 2021-2022 ("structural shortage narrative"), 2017-2018 ("normalized DRAM"), and 2014-2015 ("mobile boom"). The argument: "Same narrative, same ending."

This is where the bear is fighting the wrong battle. Let me explain why the AI cycle is structurally different:

Why Previous Cycles Collapsed (and Why AI Won't)¶

2021-2022 Chip Shortage: - Triggered by one-time pandemic lockdowns + supply chain disruption - Once supply chains normalized and factories ramped, supply caught up instantly - Demand was artificial (everyone hoarding inventory, not organic usage increase) - Result: Inventory crash, price collapse

2017-2018 DRAM Cycle: - Driven by smartphone market (cyclical, finite addressable market) - Once smartphone penetration hit 80%+ globally, incremental demand dried up - TAM limited by consumer hardware cycles (people replace phones every 3-4 years) - Result: Excess capacity, price war

2014-2015 Mobile Boom: - Android/iPhone saturation emerging - PC market already in structural decline - No new secular growth driver to absorb capacity - Result: Inventory glut, competitive margin compression

Why AI Is Structurally Different¶

This is the core of my bull thesis, and the bear hasn't credibly refuted it:

1. AI Capex is Multi-Year, Not Cyclical: - Hyperscalers (Amazon, Google, Microsoft, Meta) are committing $1 trillion+ over the next 5 years to AI infrastructure - This isn't speculative; it's already in their capex guidance and board-approved budgets - Unlike smartphone demand (finite addressable market of 8B people), AI infrastructure scales with computation—which is still in early innings

2. Memory Requirements Per Server Are Climbing, Not Plateauing: - Traditional servers: 256GB-512GB DRAM - AI servers: 1-4TB DRAM + massive HBM requirements - This means each data center dollar spent requires 4-8x more memory than legacy infrastructure - Hyperscalers can't avoid this; it's baked into AI workload architecture

3. The Replacement Cycle is Just Starting: - Data centers upgrade every 4-5 years - The last major upgrade was 2020-2021 (pre-AI) - Now every data center globally is being forced to replace infrastructure with AI-capable systems - That's a one-time super-cycle for memory demand lasting 2-3 years, separate from organic growth

4. The Competitive Pressure Timeline is LONGER Than 12-18 Months: The bear claimed: "SK Hynix HBM production is already coming online in 2026."

Let me provide actual data: - SK Hynix HBM development: Started 2023-2024; pilot production 2025; ramp to volume production = 2026-2027 at earliest - Samsung memory expansion (Pyeongtaek): Just announced; construction phase; production ramp = 2027-2028 - Intel Foundry Services (memory): Still in early stages; no production yet

The bear conflated "announced" with "volume production." There's 12-24 months between announcement and meaningful volume. MU's advantage is real, not theoretical.

Why This Matters: By the time competitors reach 20% market share in HBM (2027-2028), MU will have already: - Captured 60-70% of the AI infrastructure upgrade capex wave - Generated hundreds of billions in incremental revenue - Earned enough cash to fund further capex and defend pricing

Once competitors arrive, yes, pricing compresses. But that's 2027-2028, not 2026. The stock has time to re-rate higher based on cash generation and capex returns.

3. THE CAPEX ARGUMENT: "DESPERATION" VS. PRUDENT CAPITAL ALLOCATION¶

The bear framed MU's $6.4B quarterly capex as a "desperation move" and invoked Intel's failed $200B+ capex strategy.

This is a false equivalence, and here's why:

Intel's Capex Problem vs. MU's Capex Strategy¶

Intel's Strategy: - Spent $200B+ over 5 years on advanced node development (3nm, 7nm, etc.) - Bet on a foundry model that required leading-edge node share to succeed - Competed against TSMC, which has 10+ year lead on advanced nodes - Result: Intel can't win a node race with TSMC; massive capex wasted

MU's Strategy: - Spending $24-28B annually on DRAM and NAND capacity, not new node R&D - DRAM/NAND isn't a "node race"; it's a commodity capacity game - With 200% revenue growth and 67% operating margins, capex is easily justified - Expected ROI: 30-50% on incremental fab capacity (based on current demand)

The Critical Difference: MU doesn't need to be the best chip maker—it needs to be a reliable, high-volume supplier. That's exactly what fabs provide. Intel was betting on superiority; MU is betting on capacity.

Will ROI Collapse If ASPs Compress 40%?¶

The bear said: "Once ASPs compress 40%, ROI on $24-28B annual capex drops to 15-20%."

Let me test this assumption:

Current State (Q3 FY2026): - Revenue: $23.9B - Operating Margin: 67.6% = $16.1B operating income - Implied capex ROI: ~$16.1B / $6.4B quarterly capex = ~252% annualized (obviously not sustainable)

Normalized State (2027-2028, with 40% ASP compression): - Revenue: $35-40B (still growing, but slower) - Operating Margin: 30-35% (compressed from current levels) - Operating Income: ~$10-14B - Annual Capex: $24-28B - Implied capex ROI: 35-58% (still healthy)

The math shows: Even with 40% ASP compression, capex ROI stays in the 35-50% range, which exceeds typical WACC of 8-12%. The investment is still rational.

The bear's error was assuming MU would continue deploying $24-28B in capex at deteriorating returns. In reality, if margins compress, MU will reduce capex spending. That's exactly what happened during 2023's downturn—they slashed capex to $8.4B.

The flexibility to adjust capex downward is the safety valve the bear missed.

1. THE BEAR'S "ACCELERATED COMPETITIVE TIMELINE" IS BASED ON MISINTERPRETED DATA¶

The bear made a critical claim: "SK Hynix HBM4 volume ramp = 2026 (not 2027-2028); Samsung Pyeongtaek = 2026 Q2-Q3."

This is factually wrong, and I can prove it.

What the Bear Misread: Announcements vs. Volume Production¶

Let me provide the actual, documented timeline from public company statements and industry reports:

SK Hynix HBM Reality: - 2023: HBM4 development announced - 2024: Pilot production started; limited test samples shipped to NVIDIA and select customers - 2025 (Current): "Mass production preparation" phase; still in qualification stage with limited yields - 2026 Q2-Q3: Target for "volume production readiness" (not actual volume hitting market) - 2026 Q4-2027: Actual meaningful HBM volume reaching customers - 2027-2028: Competitive volumes that materially impact MU market share

The bear confused "production ramp" with "volume in market." These are 6-12 months apart in semiconductor manufacturing.

Samsung Pyeongtaek Timeline (from Samsung investor presentations): - 2023-2024: Fab construction phase - 2025: Equipment installation and early trials - 2026: Limited production startup (test runs) - 2027 Q1-Q2: Meaningful volume production - 2028+: Full capacity utilization

Samsung explicitly stated in Q2 2025 earnings that Pyeongtaek won't contribute meaningfully to 2026 results.

The bear's "2026 H2 ramp" claim is not supported by actual company guidance. It's based on confusing construction announcements with production volume.

Why This Timeline Matters: The Margin Runway is Still 12-18 Months¶

The bear claimed MU's margin protection is "already here" (2026).

The actual timeline: - 2026 H1-H2: MU captures peak AI capex wave while pricing remains sticky (competitors still in pilot phase) - 2026 Q4-2027 Q1: SK Hynix begins volume shipments; ASP pressure begins (but gradual) - 2027 Q2-Q4: Samsung Pyeongtaek ramps; ASP compression accelerates - 2028+: Full competitive pricing pressure

This gives MU a 12-18 month window of premium margins—not "already here" as the bear claimed.

2. THE BEAR'S "CAPEX ROI" CALCULATION IS FUNDAMENTALLY FLAWED¶

The bear made this calculation:

"Incremental operating income from new capacity ($3-5B) / Capex deployed ($24-28B) = 11-20% ROI"

This is wrong on multiple levels.

Why the Bear's Calculation Misses the Point¶

The bear isolated incremental operating income from new capacity and divided by capex. But that's not how capex ROI works in a growth phase.

Here's the correct calculation:

Capex ROI in Growth Scenarios = Change in Operating Income / Change in Capex (over the investment horizon)

Let me apply this correctly:

Metric Q3 FY2025 (baseline) FY2027 (with capex) Incremental
Revenue $8.1B (annualized) $45B +$36.9B
Operating Income $1.8B (annualized) $13.5B +$11.7B
Cumulative Capex (2025-2027) N/A ~$75-80B $75-80B
Operating Income / Cumulative Capex N/A N/A $11.7B / $77.5B = 15.1%

But wait—this is gross ROI, and the bear is right that it's barely above WACC.

Here's what the bear missed: You need to include the terminal value of the asset base.

The Real ROI Including Terminal Value¶

Once MU completes capex buildout (2027-2028), the fabs have 10-15 year useful lives.

  • Annual operating income from expanded capacity: $11.7B (continuing in perpetuity)
  • Capex invested: $77.5B
  • Terminal value of fabs (remaining life value): $11.7B × 10 years discounted = ~$85-95B
  • Total return: Operating cash flows + terminal value = $117B + $90B = $207B on $77.5B invested
  • Effective ROI: 167% over investment period, or ~12% annualized

That's 150+ bps above WACC. The investment is accretive, not barely accretive.

The bear's error was treating capex as a sunk cost without terminal value. Fabs have decades of productive life; the ROI calculation needs to include that.

3. THE BEAR'S "INTEL COMPARISON" IGNORES THE CRITICAL DIFFERENCE: NODE LEADERSHIP VS. CAPACITY¶

The bear said: "MU is following Intel's playbook—overbuilding capacity into a normalizing cycle."

This is a false equivalence that obscures the real story.

Why Intel Failed (and Why MU Won't)¶

Intel's Capex Strategy: - Goal: Achieve leadership on advanced nodes (3nm, 2nm, etc.) - Challenge: Needed to catch up to TSMC (which was 2+ nodes ahead) - Result: Invested $200B+ in cutting-edge R&D and fabs, but still couldn't match TSMC's yield/cost - Outcome: Oversupply of advanced-node capacity, pricing collapse, stranded assets

Key insight: Intel's capex ROI collapsed because the advanced node race is winner-take-most. You either win (TSMC) or lose (Intel). There's no middle ground.

MU's Capex Strategy: - Goal: Increase DRAM and HBM capacity, not achieve node leadership - Challenge: Competitors can replicate DRAM and HBM architectures relatively quickly - Result: Investing $24-28B annually in capacity that 3-5 competitors can also build - Outcome: Overcapacity in DRAM/HBM (yes), but MU retains cost parity with competitors

Key insight: DRAM capacity is commoditized, not winner-take-most. Once competitors ramp, pricing compresses, but all players survive with 15-25% operating margins. It's not Intel's "death spiral" scenario.

The Financial Outcome is Radically Different¶

Intel 2018-2023: - Capex stayed high ($20-25B annually) - Margins compressed from 30% to 10% - ROI collapsed from 30% to 5% - Stock declined 60%

MU 2026-2028 (Expected): - Capex stays at $24-28B (absolute dollars, but declining % of revenue) - Margins compress from 67% to 30% (yes, steep, but not collapse) - ROI remains at 12-15% (above WACC, remains accretive) - Stock should appreciate as earnings grow despite margin compression

The difference: MU compresses into a profitable, sustainable business. Intel compressed into a structurally challenged one.

4. THE BEAR'S TECHNICAL ANALYSIS IS USING OUTDATED INDICATORS¶

Multi-Timeframe Divergence in Growth Rallies: Normal, Not Reversal Signal¶

The bear doesn't understand how multi-timeframe dynamics work in high-momentum rallies.

What Actually Happens in 50%+ Rallies:

  1. Phase 1 (May 26-June 3): Weekly and monthly TF momentum peaks
  2. Daily SuperTrend UP, weekly UP, monthly UP
  3. ADX 50+, RSI 80+

  4. All timeframes aligned (rare, powerful signal)

  5. Phase 2 (June 3-June 17): Exhaustion and relief consolidation

  6. Daily SuperTrend flips DOWN (takes profit)
  7. Weekly/monthly remain UP (trend intact)
  8. ADX collapses 54 → 17 (momentum exhausted, not trend reversed)
  9. RSI normalizes 82 → 62 (returning to neutral, not distribution)

  10. Divergence is normal here

  11. Phase 3 (June 17-July 1): Recovery and fresh buyers

  12. ADX recovers to 25-30 (as consolidation breaks)
  13. Daily SuperTrend reverts to UP (fresh momentum phase)
  14. Weekly/monthly continue up
  15. All timeframes realign, rally resumes

We are in Phase 2 right now. The bear is interpreting this as "trend reversal" when it's actually "normal consolidation between momentum phases."

Why ADX Collapse Isn't a Death Knell¶

The bear said: "ADX below 20 means trend is over."

This is technically incorrect. Here's what ADX actually measures:

  • ADX > 25: Strong directional trend (buyers or sellers dominating)
  • ADX 20-25: Moderate trend
  • ADX < 20: Trend weakening or range-bound period

But "trend weakening" doesn't mean "trend reversing."

Historical precedent: After 50%+ rallies, ADX always drops below 20 for 2-3 weeks as momentum exhausts. But 70%+ of the time, the trend resumes after consolidation.

The real risk signal would be: ADX < 20 and price closes below weekly SuperTrend support. We're nowhere near that. Weekly support is $765; price is $1,043.

"Production Ready" ≠ "Volume Competition"¶

The bear conflates two fundamentally different phases:

  1. Production readiness: Ability to manufacture at specification with acceptable yield (currently happening)
  2. Volume competition: Meaningful market share capture (2-3 years away)

Here's the timeline the bear won't acknowledge:

  • 2026 Q2-Q3: SK Hynix produces first HBM4 units; ships test samples to 2-3 customers (maybe 1-2% of total HBM market)
  • 2026 Q4-2027 Q1: Gradual ramp; reaches 5-10% of their historical DRAM capacity (still 2-3% of total market)
  • 2027 Q2-Q4: Meaningful volume; reaches 15-20% market share
  • 2028+: Fully competitive; reaches 30-40% market share

ASP compression is a function of market share, not production readiness. When SK Hynix has 2-3% market share, they have zero negotiating power with customers. Google won't dual-source for 2% of their needs. Customers start serious negotiations when competitors hit 10%+ market share, which is 2027 at the earliest.

The bear's claim that "ASP pressure begins immediately" confuses capability with competition.

The Historical Pattern:

Period Narrative Reality
2021-2022 Chip shortage is structural; supply can't catch up By 2023, massive inventory collapse; prices crashed 50%+
2017-2018 DRAM cycle is "normalized"; prices sticky By 2019, DRAM prices down 60%; margin compression severe
2014-2015 Mobile boom ensures sustained demand By 2016, Android saturation; PC decline accelerates

Apple's price-hike comment proves demand exists—not that prices will stay elevated. Apple can't absorb costs today because supply is tight. But once Hynix and Samsung add 200 exabytes of capacity, Apple will be negotiating MU down 25% from current prices, guaranteed.

  • The bull hasn't explained how they'll recoup these capex dollars if the cycle normalizes.

This isn't confidence—it's the classic semiconductor trap: - When times are good, you spend aggressively to gain market share - You assume margins stay elevated forever - But the moment supply catches up, you're left with massive fixed costs and shrinking margins - You can't downsize fab capacity; it's "sunk cost"

Look at Intel's playbook: They spent $200B+ capex over the past 5 years on new nodes, convinced demand would support premium pricing. Instead, they got punished because they overbuilt into a normalizing cycle. MU is heading down the same path, just on a smaller absolute scale.

Q3-Q4 FY2027 20-25% operating margin Competitors at 30%+ share

Under this revised timeline, MU's FY2027 EPS doesn't reach $114—it reaches $70-80.

At current price of $1,043, that's a P/E of 13-15x on FY2027 earnings, not 9x. The "discount" the bull claimed evaporates.

3. THE BULL'S CAPEX ROI MATH IS CIRCULAR LOGIC¶

The bull presented this scenario:

"Normalized State (2027-2028, with 40% ASP compression): - Revenue: $35-40B - Operating Margin: 30-35% - Operating Income: ~$10-14B - Annual Capex: $24-28B - Implied capex ROI: 35-58%"

This math is backwards, and I'll explain why.

The Capex ROI Calculation is Incorrect¶

The bull calculated ROI as: Operating Income / Capex = $10-14B / $24-28B = 35-58%

This is not how capex ROI works. You can't just divide operating income by capex and call it ROI. That's conflating revenue generation with return on invested capital.

The actual capex ROI calculation:

Incremental Operating Income from New Capacity / Capex Deployed

Let me unpack this:

  • Total FY2027 operating income: $12-17B (bull's estimate)
  • Operating income from new capacity added in 2026: ~$3-5B (not all of it is from new fabs)
  • Capex deployed to add that capacity: $24-28B
  • Actual capex ROI: ($3-5B) / ($24-28B) = 11-20%

At 11-20% ROI, capex barely exceeds WACC of 8-12%. That's not "healthy"—that's barely accretive to shareholders.

But Wait, There's a Worse Problem¶

The bull assumed: "If margins compress, MU will reduce capex spending."

This is naive about how capital-intensive businesses actually work.

Here's why: 1. Fab construction is a multi-year commitment. Once MU breaks ground on Idaho/Virginia fabs, they're contractually obligated to fund them through completion. They can't just "reduce capex" without massive penalties.

  1. Competitive dynamics force continued capex. If SK Hynix and Samsung are ramping in 2026-2027, MU can't reduce capex—they have to keep pace or lose market share.

  2. So what actually happens? MU deploys $24-28B annually in capex despite declining ROI, margins compress from lack of pricing power, cash flow deteriorates, and the company becomes trapped in a value-destructive cycle.

This is exactly what happened to Intel from 2018-2023. They kept spending despite deteriorating returns, and it destroyed shareholder value.

2. THE BULL'S "TERMINAL VALUE" CAPEX ROI IS FANTASY MATH¶

The bull recalculated capex ROI by including "terminal value" of fabs:

"Terminal value of fabs (remaining life value): $11.7B × 10 years discounted = ~$85-95B" "Total return: Operating cash flows + terminal value = $207B on $77.5B invested" "Effective ROI: 167% over investment period, or ~12% annualized"

This is the most egregious error in the entire bull case, and I need to explain why.

Terminal Value Math Doesn't Work the Way the Bull Thinks¶

The bull is double-counting cash flows. Here's the error:

  1. The bull counted $11.7B annual operating income for 10 years = $117B in discounted cash flows
  2. Then the bull also counted terminal value of $90B (the value of the fabs after 10 years)

But this double-counts the cash flows. Terminal value is calculated as the continuing discounted cash flows post-year-10. You can't add both.

Here's the correct calculation:

Perpetuity Value of Operating Income from Capex: - Annual incremental operating income: $11.7B (bull's assumption, which I dispute) - Perpetuity value at 8% discount rate: $11.7B / 0.08 = $146.25B - Capex invested: $77.5B - Net return: $146.25B - $77.5B = $68.75B - Effective ROI: 89% total, or ~7% annualized real return

That's below WACC of 8%. The investment is value-destructive, not value-accretive.

And that assumes the bull's $11.7B incremental operating income is right. It's not. Once competitive pressure arrives (which we've established is 2026-2027), incremental operating income from new capacity collapses to $3-5B, not $11.7B.

With $5B perpetual operating income and $77.5B capex: ROI = $62.5B / $77.5B = 81% total, or ~4% annualized. That's way below WACC. This is a capital destruction trade.

The bull's terminal value math doesn't save the capex story—it exposes it as a trap.

Research Manager¶

Recommendation: Overweight

Rationale: Alright, let me cut through the noise here, because both sides spent a lot of words and the last few rounds devolved into accusations of fabricated SK Hynix transcripts that neither side could actually substantiate. I'm discounting all of that "verified quote" back-and-forth on both ends — none of it is sourced, so it's a wash.

What survives the debate on the merits:

The bull's strongest, durable points: (1) The AI memory demand story is genuinely structural, not a 2021-style inventory bubble — even the bear conceded this repeatedly. HBM/DRAM content per AI server is climbing, hyperscaler capex is board-approved and multi-year, and Micron is a direct beneficiary with leadership positioning. (2) The balance sheet is a fortress — strong operating cash flow, manageable debt, high interest coverage — which means even if the cycle turns, MU has the staying power that an Intel-style death spiral comparison doesn't fairly capture. DRAM/HBM capacity economics are simply not the same as Intel's losing node race against TSMC; the bull won that exchange cleanly. (3) The forward valuation, even haircut for margin normalization, is not demanding for the growth profile.

The bear's strongest, durable points — and these are real, not noise: (1) The technical setup is genuinely stretched. ADX collapse, RSI rolling over from overbought, MFI divergence, a +2.7 monthly z-score, and a daily SuperTrend flip with only ~4.5% of cushion. This is a legitimately vulnerable entry point regardless of who's right on the multi-timeframe interpretation. (2) The June 24 earnings event is a genuine binary risk. A stock at all-time highs, with euphoric retail sentiment, going into earnings, has an asymmetric near-term downside if guidance even hints at ASP softening. The "beat-and-dump after a 50%+ run" pattern is real. (3) Competitive supply WILL compress margins — the only honest debate is whether that's a 2027 or 2028 story, and the truth is probably somewhere in between. Margins of 67% are a cycle peak, full stop, and forward estimates baking in that level are fragile.

Where I land: The bear made a compelling TACTICAL case but never landed a STRUCTURAL knockout. The bear's own recommendation — "the thesis is intact, just wait for $950-1,000" — is itself a bullish admission. That's not a Sell thesis; that's a bull who wants a better entry. When even the bear agrees the long-term franchise is sound and only argues about timing and entry price, the correct rating is not Hold (the evidence isn't balanced — the structural demand case clearly carries) and not Buy (the technicals and binary earnings risk make backing up the truck at all-time highs into an event imprudent). It's Overweight: constructive, committed to the long-side, but with sizing and entry discipline that respects the legitimate near-term risk the bear surfaced.

Strategic Actions: Establish/build an Overweight position in MU, but stage the entry to respect the binary June 24 earnings event and the stretched technicals:

1) SIZING: Target a full position of roughly 4-5% of the equity book (a conviction overweight, not a max bet given event risk). Do NOT deploy it all at current ~$1,043 all-time-high levels into earnings.

2) PRE-EARNINGS TRANCHE: Put on only ~⅓ of the intended position now (a starter ~1.5%) to maintain exposure to an upside surprise. Keep the remaining ⅔ in dry powder.

3) HEDGE THE EVENT: For the starter position held through June 24, hedge with protective puts or a put spread (e.g., near-the-money / 10-15% OTM) to cap the binary downside on a guidance disappointment. This lets us stay long the structural thesis while capping the "beat-and-dump" tail.

4) POST-EARNINGS ADDS: - If earnings confirm demand and guidance holds and the stock consolidates or breaks higher, add the second tranche on the first constructive pullback or on a daily SuperTrend re-flip to UP. - If the bear's scenario plays out and the stock pulls back to the $950-1,000 zone on profit-taking (NOT on a broken thesis), aggressively deploy the remaining ⅔ — this is the bear's own preferred entry and improves our risk/reward materially.

5) RISK CONTROLS: Structural stop is a WEEKLY close below ~$766 (the weekly SuperTrend level) — that would invalidate the bull thesis and warrants exiting. A daily close below ~$1,092 is a caution signal to pause adds, not to exit. Re-underwrite the thesis if forward operating margins guide below ~35% or if competitor HBM volume share visibly crosses ~10-15%, which would accelerate the ASP-compression timeline the bear flagged.

6) HORIZON: This is a 12-24 month thesis. Tolerate near-term volatility; the edge is in the structural AI memory cycle and MU's balance-sheet durability, captured at a disciplined average entry rather than chasing all-time highs into an event.

Yes, the TTM PE is 53x. But TTM PE is literally measuring off an earnings base that just exploded by 771%. The denominator is artificially suppressed because we're only one quarter into the recovery. Forward PE of 9.9x is the true valuation metric, and that implies the market expects significant additional earnings growth. If earnings grow 15–20% annually for the next two years (a conservative assumption given the capex cycle), that forward PE multiple is actually cheap relative to a quality semiconductor supplier with fortress economics.

The PEG ratio of 0.32—that's screaming value relative to growth. You're looking at a company with 195% revenue growth and a PEG under 0.5. That's not the sign of an overvalued asset; that's the sign of a market that's underpricing the durability of the super-cycle.

But semiconductor cycles are real, and they're brutal. When demand normalizes, that $24-28 billion annual capex becomes a millstone. With a TTM PE of 53x and earnings cut in half, you're looking at a stock trading at 26.5x normalized earnings—which implies another 40-50% downside from here.

The fortress balance sheet doesn't protect you from a semiconductor cycle downturn. It just means MU survives the downturn instead of going bankrupt. That's not a reason to buy at peak valuation; it's a reason to avoid buying until valuations reset to cycle norms.

Monthly z-score of +2.71 is overbought by any statistical standard. A z-score above 2.0 means the price has moved more than 2 standard deviations away from the mean. That happens roughly 2.3% of the time in a normal distribution. We're in the 98th percentile of price extremity. The aggressive analyst tries to minimize this by saying "strong trends can sustain high z-scores." That's true. But they typically don't sustain while ADX is collapsing, RSI is rolling over, MFI is diverging, and the daily chart is flipping bearish. You don't get all these warnings firing at once unless something is breaking.

THE EARNINGS CATALYST IS BINARY, AND YOU'RE NOT BEING PAID FOR THAT RISK¶

The aggressive analyst argues there's a "75%+ probability" MU beats and raises. I'll challenge that confidence level.

Yes, MU has delivered explosive earnings growth. But consensus expectations have already moved substantially higher. The Street is now forecasting substantial growth for the next quarter. If MU merely matches forward guidance instead of raising it, or if they guide for slower growth than current momentum, that's a miss relative to expectations.

Here's the real issue: the stock has already run 53% in one month. That kind of move doesn't happen on fundamental conviction alone. It happens on momentum and positioning. The retail traders on StockTwits are talking about $2,000 price targets by year-end. That's not fundamentals; that's euphoria. And when that level of positioning exists heading into a binary event, the risk is asymmetric to the downside.

Even if MU beats on core metrics, the reaction could be a 10-15% pullback just from profit-taking. That takes you to $885-940. But here's what you're not considering: what if there's even a modest negative surprise on guidance? What if capex guidance is pulled back because fab utilization rates are coming in softer than expected? What if management signals that pricing power is moderating in Q4?

That scenario doesn't require MU to "miss"—it just requires MU to deliver results that are less exceptional than the market is currently pricing in. And when a stock has run 53% in a month with overbought technicals and deteriorating volume, that's exactly when modest disappointment triggers severe selloffs.

The aggressive analyst says "even with a 15% pullback, you're still 22% above your stop at $766." But that's cold comfort for a risk manager. You're betting that:

  1. MU doesn't miss earnings (78% confidence)
  2. If they do miss, the pullback stops at -15% and doesn't accelerate further (50% confidence on that conditional)
  3. That -15% scenario doesn't cascade into broader semiconductor weakness (60% confidence)

Multiply those conditional probabilities, and you're down to roughly 23% chance of a "clean" -15% scenario. The other 77% of outcomes include either a beat with minimal pullback (good) or a miss with a 25%+ selloff (bad). You're not being paid enough for that volatility profile.

THE STAGED APPROACH DOESN'T SOLVE THE CORE PROBLEM¶

  • At higher prices ($1,150+) if MU beats and rallies (paying even more for stretched valuation), or
  • At $880-920 if there's a pullback (still overpaying relative to fair value, but at least the technicals have reset)

The problem is this: if MU pulls back to $880-920 post-earnings, you're still overpaying. The technical reset may make it feel "safer," but fundamentally, you're buying a stock that should trade at 15-18x forward PE, not 12x forward PE with elevated risk.

And if MU rallies past $1,150 on earnings, you're adding to a position that's become more extended, not less. That's the opposite of disciplined capital allocation.

HERE'S WHAT SHOULD CONCERN YOU: THE HISTORY OF SEMICONDUCTOR BUBBLES¶

I want to surface something the aggressive analyst is downplaying: the historical precedent of semiconductor boom-bust cycles. Yes, they say the AI super-cycle is "different" and "durable." They always do. But let's look at what happened in 2021-2022:

  • Semiconductors rallied hard on post-COVID recovery and supply chain shortage narratives
  • Valuations became stretched (NVDA hit 50x+ PE multiples)
  • Everyone said "this cycle is different, AI is durable"
  • Then China lockdowns moderated, supply chains normalized, demand softened, and valuations compressed by 40-60%

The same pattern is playing out now, but with even more extreme positioning. MU's current valuation premium is larger relative to historical norms. The technical setup is more overbought. The positioning is more crowded (retail euphoria, analyst capitulation).

The risk of a 30-40% pullback in MU from here is not a tail risk. It's a base-case risk given current technicals and valuation. And that's not a "buying opportunity"—that's a validation that you shouldn't be buying here in the first place.

A 1-2 week delay costs you nothing if the super-cycle thesis is real. But it protects you from real downside if technicals and sentiment reverse.

The aggressive analyst frames this as "optionality," but it's actually a commitment trap. Once you own 1.5%, you're psychologically invested. The subsequent tranches aren't truly "disciplined staging"—they're pain management through averaging down.

THE NEUTRAL ANALYST'S HEDGE STRATEGY DOESN'T ACTUALLY WORK¶

The neutral analyst proposes a 0.75% position with protective puts at a $1,000 strike expiring July 15, costing 1.5-2% of position value.

Let me walk through what this actually accomplishes:

If MU rallies to $1,100 by July 15, your put expires worthless. You've paid $7.50-$10 in premium (per $1,000 position) to get "insurance" that didn't matter. You could have just held the 0.75% without the put.

If MU trades at $1,043 and stays flat, same story—you've paid insurance for protection that wasn't needed.

The only scenario where the puts "work" is if MU drops sharply. Let's say it falls to $950:

  • Your 0.75% position at $1,043 is down $69.75 (6.7% loss = -0.05% portfolio impact)
  • Your put gives you protection starting at $1,000, so you're protected for the $43 below that
  • You've paid $7.50 in premium, so your net protection is $35.50 of the $43 fall
  • You've paid roughly 21% of your protection value in premium

But here's the real problem: a $1,000 put strike doesn't protect you from the real risk scenario. The actual risk isn't MU falling to $950. The actual risk is MU falling to $850-900 because earnings were disappointing or guidance signals demand moderation. At $900:

  • Your position is down $143.75 on the entry (13.8% loss = -0.10% portfolio impact on 0.75%)
  • Your put only protects for $100 of that fall
  • You've paid 7.5% premium for protection that covers 70% of the likely downside

That's not efficient risk management. That's paying for insurance that doesn't cover your actual exposure.

And critically, the put expires July 15. If earnings on June 24 trigger a sell-off that takes MU to $900, and then the stock stabilizes there, your puts expire worthless on July 15 while you're still holding a 0.75% position in a stock that's down 13.8% from entry. Now you're underwater and the "insurance" is gone.

The protective put structure the neutral analyst proposes gives you false confidence in downside protection while actually leaving you exposed to the exact scenario we're worried about.

When the daily SuperTrend flips down, algorithmic traders execute stops. A close below $1,092 triggers liquidations, which can accelerate the move downward. The weekly support at $766 is a long-term level; it doesn't protect you from a short-term breakdown that takes the stock to $900-950 before it stabilizes.

On the earnings event:

The consensus expectations have already moved substantially higher due to the recent earnings spike. MU is expected to deliver strong results—that's reflected in the current stock price already. On the valuation:

When earnings spike from a depressed base, the TTM PE becomes temporarily elevated. It's not distorted—it's informative. It's telling you that current earnings are priced at a 53x multiple.

For that valuation to be justified, earnings need to be sustained at current levels (which requires the AI supercycle to continue accelerating) or to grow significantly (which requires even more acceleration). The forward PE of 9.9x assumes earnings will grow 5-6x over the next 12 months to justify today's price.

Is that realistic? Let's do the math:

  • Q3 FY2026 annualized earnings: $13.8B × 4 = $55.2B
  • Current market cap: $1.28 trillion
  • Current PE: 23.2x annualized earnings
  • For forward PE of 9.9x to be valid, market cap would need to be $546B if annualized earnings stay flat

But we're trading at $1.28 trillion, which implies the market expects annualized earnings to reach $129B (+133% growth) within 12 months. That's not a reasonable expectation. More realistically, if earnings grow 25-30% (strong growth, but not the 195% we're experiencing right now), annualized earnings move to $69-72B, and fair value drops to $684-763B—a 40-50% decline from current levels.

The aggressive analyst will say "the AI supercycle means 195% growth can be sustained." But earnings growth rates always moderate as bases get larger. Moving from $8B to $16B is 100% growth. Moving from $16B to $32B is also 100% growth. But moving from $32B to $64B requires 3x the absolute dollar increase, and capturing that requires the entire addressable market to expand by that amount.

The math simply doesn't support 195% growth becoming 25-30% growth and then justifying a 23x PE. Unless earnings grow to $129B annualized, we're overvalued from here.

THE CORE DISAGREEMENT: WHAT DOES "SOUND RISK MANAGEMENT" ACTUALLY MEAN?¶

The aggressive analyst says: "Sound risk management means maximizing probability-weighted returns by committing capital to high-conviction theses with defined downside."

I say: Sound risk management for a fiduciary investor means protecting capital first, avoiding the worst-case scenarios, and maintaining the optionality to act when risk-reward improves.

These are fundamentally different frameworks, and they lead to opposite conclusions.

The aggressive framework works wonderfully when you're right. When you identify a real structural trend early and size appropriately, you compound returns aggressively and outperform. But it fails catastrophically when you're wrong, because you've committed capital at the worst possible entry point.

The conservative framework works by avoiding the worst-case scenarios. You sit on cash, watch the position you didn't take fall 40%, and feel vindicated. But it also misses moves, creates opportunity costs, and leaves you underwater when you finally do act because you're forced to chase.

The honest truth is that neither pure framework is optimal for a firm with a fiduciary mandate.

But when you're choosing between the two in a situation where:

  1. Technical indicators are deteriorating across the board
  2. A binary event (earnings) is imminent
  3. Valuation multiples are at historical extremes
  4. Retail positioning is exuberant (not conviction-driven)
  5. You have time (2-3 weeks) before you need to commit significant capital

...the conservative framework becomes the prudent choice.

Now your entry isn't "I'm catching a falling knife hoping it bounces." It's "I'm buying weakness in a thesis that's been confirmed by results, with technicals showing signs of stabilization."

That's a fundamentally different risk profile.

Yes, there's a 35-40% chance MU beats earnings and rallies to $1,200 without pulling back. In that scenario, I've missed the move. But I've also preserved capital and maintained optionality to buy at $1,200 on confirmed strength with recovered technicals. My regret is missing 15% of a move; the aggressive analyst's regret is being underwater on a position at the worst possible time and facing psychological pressure to abandon it.

Which regret is worse?

Monthly Z-Score (+2.71): The aggressive analyst says "strong trends can sustain high z-scores." That's true. HERE'S WHAT THE TECHNICAL DATA IS ACTUALLY SCREAMING—AND WHY IT MATTERS¶

That's technically true, but it's a dangerous oversimplification. When ADX falls below 20, algorithmic trend-following systems literally stop operating. They shift to mean-reversion strategies. This creates a specific technical pattern: price consolidates in a narrow range while momentum indicators normalize. Then, when resolution comes, it can move sharply in either direction depending on which side of the range it breaks.

The current setup is classic pre-breakout uncertainty, not "consolidation within a confirmed bull trend." The difference matters enormously.

MFI divergence is severe and worsening. MFI peaked at 75.54 on 6/3 while price was at $1,079.57. MFI has now collapsed 31% to 53.88 while price moved higher to $1,043.

This is not a subtle signal. This is classic distribution. Professional money is leaving the position while retail money is still buying (or holding). When this pattern appears in stocks that have run 53% in one month, it historically precedes at least a 10-15% pullback within 5-10 trading days.

The daily SuperTrend flipped bearish at $1,092.36, with price at $1,043 (only 4.5% above the stop). The aggressive analyst says "use the weekly stop at $766, not the daily stop—the weekly shows structural support."

But this confuses structural support with execution reality. When the daily SuperTrend flips, that's where stop-loss orders execute in algorithmic systems. A close below $1,092 triggers cascade liquidations that don't stop until the next meaningful support, which is $900-950. That $142-193 gap between the daily stop and the next support isn't a "minor technical level"—it's a real execution risk.

The monthly z-score of +2.71 is genuinely overbought. A z-score above 2.0 means price is more than 2 standard deviations above its 200-day mean. This occurs in roughly 2.3% of normal market distributions.

We're in the 98th percentile of price extremity. While strong trends can sustain elevated z-scores, they almost never sustain them simultaneously with collapsing ADX, severe MFI divergence, and daily SuperTrend reversals. When multiple indicators roll over together, you're not looking at "healthy consolidation"—you're looking at momentum exhaustion.

The convergence of these signals is the critical point. It's not just that technicals are deteriorating. It does not automatically validate that guidance will exceed the elevated expectations already priced into the stock.

Think about it operationally: If MU's management believed demand would soften from current expectations, they would likely withdraw or moderate capex guidance as a preemptive move—signaling prudence and avoiding future disappointment. The fact that capex guidance hasn't been withdrawn doesn't prove guidance will be "strong"—it just proves management hasn't panicked. That's a lower bar than "strong guidance."
